Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. | **[TamperCert: A Benchmark for Detecting Text Tampering in Degree Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30419v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Degree certificates play a critical role in high-stakes decisions such as university admission, immigration, and hiring. Reported cases show otherwise: in 2024, the business school of the University of Hong Kong found that about 30 master's students had enrolled with forged academic credentials. We present TamperCert, a benchmark built from 400 real degree certificates issued by 38 universities. Each certificate was subjected to 11 publicly available tampering methods to alter the holder's name and further processed with four additional post-processing techniques, resulting in over 20,000 forged images. We evaluate 13 detectors, a commercial multimodal large language model (MLLM), and detectors trained on TamperCert.Our findings reveal that no detector is reliable for every tampering method. Consequently, a determined forger may still evade detection. Moreover, the commercial MLLM misses most forgeries made by general-purpose generative models, and post-processing, such as resizing an image or photographing it with a camera, severely degrades detection.</p></details> |  |
| **[Face Is Not All You Need: MIME Benchmark for Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30288v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) is a fundamental task in multimedia understanding, where state-of-the-art methods have achieved prominent success relying on the assumption of complete, well-aligned multimodal inputs. However, real-world unconstrained scenarios often suffer from unpredictable modality degradation and information loss, leading to catastrophic performance collapse of existing MER models that heavily depend on facial cues as the core inference anchor. However, few systematic works evaluate model robustness under diverse, fine-grained incomplete settings, as prior works are limited to coarse-grained full-modality removal and neglect interpretable analysis of emotional reasoning. To bridge this gap, we formally define the Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition (IMER) task, which benchmarks model generalization and robustness under multi-grained modality and emotional information loss. We further construct MIME, a dedicated IMER benchmark with 2,000 video segments covering realistic fine-grained facial-detail degradation, severe facial degradation, and extreme full-modality loss, surpassing the limited coarse-grained settings of prior works. We also propose the Chain of Emotion (CoE) analysis paradigm with tailored metrics to dissect the fine-grained emotional cue perception and reasoning process of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Extensive experiments on seven mainstream MLLMs reveal that body language and scene context act as critical compensatory mechanisms under facial degradation, while a pronounced gap exists between models' basic visual perception ability and high-level cognitive reasoning. | **[TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. | **[PhysAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Reliable Remote Heart Rate Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00066v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables non-contact heart-rate estimation from facial videos, but its weak physiological signal is easily corrupted by motion, illumination changes, occlusion, skin-appearance variation, and device noise. To resolve these conflicts, we propose PhysAgent, an inference-time multi-agent candidate-verification framework. Unlike direct prediction approaches, PhysAgent neither trains a new base rPPG model nor asks Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to output heart rate directly. A deterministic physiological verifier checks the fusion proposal, and a reproducible numerical fusion process produces the final heart rate. Experimental results on multiple public rPPG benchmarks show that PhysAgent improves fusion stability and reliability across different datasets and source-domain settings, while avoiding the irreproducibility and physiological inconsistency of direct MLLM prediction or unconstrained ensemble fusion. | **[Divide and Conquer: A Hybrid Strategy Defeats Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16555v6)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied in various fields of society due to their powerful reasoning, understanding, and generation capabilities. On static decisions, the best System-1 model leads the best MLLM by 4.9 percentage points on images and is within one point of it on video and audio. | **[CT-Navigator: Towards Interpretable and Generalizable CT Understanding through Agentic Perception and Tool-Invocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29305v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical computed tomography (CT) interpretation inherently requires progressive visual exploration, where radiologists navigate from whole scans to individual slices and regions of interest and interact with external tools when necessary. Existing approaches primarily scale Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in language, forcing inherently spatial or visual computations into discrete text tokens. We further introduce a reinforcement learning stage that optimizes rationale trajectories using both answer evidence and LVIP-based latent grounding, encouraging reasoning paths that remain visually consistent. To evaluate the cognitive capabilities of MLLMs, we present CogSense-Bench, a comprehensive visual question answering (VQA) benchmark assessing five cognitive dimensions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MLLMs trained with Cognitive Supersensing significantly outperform state-of-the-art baselines on CogSense-Bench and exhibit superior generalization on out-of-domain mathematics and science VQA benchmarks, suggesting that internal visual imagery may be key to bridging the gap between perceptual recognition and cognitive understanding. Causal analyses through noise injection, latent patching, and linear probing show that LVIP states progressively encode task-relevant visual structure and influence downstream answer probabilities, supporting their role as active reasoning signals.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Learning from Another Angle: Rotation-Based Test-Time Training for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28972v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on vision-language tasks but often remain vulnerable to distribution shifts due to their fixed parameters during inference. | **[Let Androids Dream: From Visual Elements to Emergent Implications via Contextual Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17019v3)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image metaphor understanding differs from general VQA: the implied meaning is rarely explicit in pixels and instead emerges from the compositional interaction of visual elements together with social intent and culture-specific background. We then propose Let Androids Dream (LAD), a training-free framework that operationalizes contextual alignment through test-time computation: (1) Perception extracts compositional visual structure, (2) Search adaptively acquires context from parametric knowledge or web search, and (3) Reasoning aligns compositions with context to derive emergent implications. Project page: https://openraiser.github.io/StudioBench/ and code: https://github.com/openraiser/StudioBench . Equal contribution: Xinglong Xu, Yuxia Zhu. | **[HighwayVerse: A Chain-of-Inspection Benchmark for Highway Infrastructure Anomaly Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28800v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Practical highway infrastructure inspection entails progressive diagnostic reasoning from visual perception to maintenance decisions, yet existing benchmarks remain confined to isolated tasks, leaving Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) insufficiently evaluated across sequential inspection stages. PADS then periodically estimates category-level observation effects from recent policy rollouts and dynamically reallocates the training distribution toward questions where visual observations provide informative learning signals, while optimizing solely for final-answer correctness. SAME also introduces adaptive expert activation to freeze selected experts during training, reducing redundant computation and cross-task interference. | **[FM4SAR-Bench 1.0: Towards A Fair Benchmark for Evaluating Foundation Models for SAR Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27158v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models have advanced SAR image interpretation but created a heterogeneous, fragmented model ecosystem. Existing models differ substantially in their input, training data, objectives, architectures, and release policy, yet the field lacks a unified taxonomy and comparative framework. Current evaluations rely primarily on task-specific benchmarks, which are ill-suited to fair comparison among models with fundamentally different input-output formats and task paradigms, and often overlook data transparency, reproducibility, interpretability, and deployment readiness. Limited SAR data accessibility also means high reported performance does not guarantee access to training data, code, or weights, limiting reproduction, benchmarking, and deployment. To address these issues, we propose a systematic taxonomy and hierarchical evaluation framework for SAR-related foundation models: five rating levels, six dimensions, and fine-grained criteria, with separate protocols for VFMs and VLMs. Beyond overall ratings, it yields interpretable multidimensional capability profiles and supports user-defined weighting, so rankings follow user priorities. | **[DailySafe: Benchmarking Dynamic Safety Awareness in Everyday Multi-Turn Multimodal Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26298v2)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) become increasingly integrated into everyday life, their ability to provide safe and reliable assistance in realistic daily activities remains insufficiently explored. Existing safety benchmarks primarily focus on isolated harmful requests or adversarial scenarios, overlooking the safety risks that may arise as ordinary tasks unfold. In practice, users pursue legitimate goals through multi-turn, multimodal interactions, where seemingly benign requests may become safety-critical when considered alongside earlier actions and visual observations. This calls for Dynamic Safety Awareness, the ability to track evolving task conditions, recognize implicit contextual risks, and provide safe and helpful assistance. To systematically evaluate this capability, we introduce DailySafe, a benchmark comprising 195 naturalistic multi-turn, multi-image interactions across 10 everyday domains and 42 task categories. Grounded in authentic user dialogue topics and documented safety knowledge, DailySafe captures realistic scenarios where safety-critical evidence is progressively revealed across conversational turns and visual observations. Using case-specific safety annotations, DailySafe assesses whether MLLMs can recognize and appropriately address evolving risks while supporting users' legitimate goals, bringing multimodal safety evaluation closer to real-world everyday AI assistance. DailySafe is publicly available at: https://github.com/XuankunRong/DailySafe.</p></details> |  |
| **[Moment-Video: Diagnosing Temporal Fidelity of Video MLLMs on Momentary Visual Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02522v3)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in general and long-form video understanding, yet their ability to capture and use brief, answer-critical visual evidence remains underexplored. ## Open Vocabulary Segmentation - 100 in Total
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Segment Anything In-Context via Visual Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29896v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present the Segment Anything Model via Visual Demonstrations (SAYD), a foundation model for in-context segmentation. SAYD generalizes to previously-unseen segmentation tasks, including those requiring tacit visual knowledge or involving concepts that are difficult to articulate unambiguously in language. We train SAYD on 12 public datasets originally curated for panoptic segmentation using our proposed techniques, including a lightweight mask decoder for prompt-agnostic mask generation and a learning mechanism for recognizing novel query classes absent in the prompts. These innovations enhance both computational efficiency and out-of-domain generalization. Consequently, SAYD significantly outperforms existing methods of in-context segmentation and open-vocabulary segmentation across 24 out-of-domain benchmarks, spanning natural, biological, ecological, cosmological, medical, pathological, and satellite imagery.</p></details> |  |
| **[PartPO: Part-Level Policy Optimization for Shape-to-Program Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29213v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present PartPO, a reinforcement learning method for generating structured shape programs from 3D point clouds. Existing shape-to-program models are trained with supervised learning on procedurally generated data and generalize poorly to more diverse shapes, while reinforcement learning from execution feedback assigns a single scalar reward to a long program that builds many parts, making credit assignment difficult. PartPO instead builds on a core property of the task: shape programs are self-describing, with each semantically named part produced by its own code block. It segments each generated program by its parts and applies part-level rewards only to the code of the corresponding part, a category reward only to the object name, and a shape-level reward to the whole program. The required part supervision is obtained without manual annotation, by grounding part names on target meshes with an open-vocabulary segmentation model prompt-tuned on the model's own outputs. Applied to MeshCoder, PartPO improves reconstruction accuracy and execution success on ShapeNetCore and ABO over the base model, rejection-sampling fine-tuning and trajectory-level RL, and generalizes to the unseen HSSD dataset. Code will be released at https://github.com/HannuoZ/PartPO.</p></details> |  |

Despite large shifts in absolute mIoU, most of the reported improvement of later CLIP-only methods over SCLIP remains under the unified protocol (roughly 79% of the published gain). Decomposing official background handling shows that it combines numeric settings and class-name files whose dominant component reverses across benchmarks, while no tested tuning-free rule transfers reliably across all datasets. Moreover, exact collisions between background words and target names---present in six of seven official COCO-Object configurations through "bed"---make affected classes unpredictable under official post-processing, while the main per-class drops under language-model-generated vocabularies on VOC21 are largely attributable to a few generated terms. Submitted to Computer Vision and Image Understanding. Code: https://github.com/WendongYao/ovss-background-audit ; data: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24338613</p></details> |

| **[Calibrate Once, Grow the Vocabulary: Candidate-Independent Scores for Conformal Risk Control in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20715v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary segmenters accept class names at inference. We remove this dependence without training by scoring each label against a fixed reference bank of names. A label's score then stays the same when names are added, so one calibration keeps its guarantee as the vocabulary grows. The guarantee holds whatever names are added, even ones chosen per image after seeing it. | **[Grounding Object-to-Object Hazard Reasoning into 3D Risk Fields for Task-Conditioned Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17566v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safety in robotics does not solely depend on geometric constraints, but also on semantics: the given task, environment properties, and interactions between objects in the scene. We provide the VLM a structured safety tool based on an RGB-D observation and a natural-language task instruction that first decides whether the task should be rejected since its execution may result in a non-recoverable hazardous outcome. Otherwise, it identifies preventable object-pair hazards and specifies the spatial relations that must be avoided. Open-vocabulary segmentation and depth information are then used to ground the detected objects in 3D and construct continuous risk fields that represent the corresponding hazardous regions. These fields are integrated with an obstacle avoidance module that corrects nominal robot motions during execution while accounting for the risk field. We evaluate the framework on preventable hazards, tasks requiring rejection, and safe variants in simulation and the real-world. Project website: https://vlm-hazard-riskfield.github.io</p></details> |  |
| **[Lang-SVG: Hierarchical Image Vectorization with Language Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15941v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image vectorization reconstructs raster images as compact scalable vector graph- ics (SVG), providing a structured and editable code representation of images. Lang-SVG incorporates granularity-controllable and open-vocabulary segmentation priors to initialize object-part hierarchies and leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with SVG tree context to assign semantic labels to unlabeled nodes. To reduce redundancy, we introduce depth-adaptive simplification to reduce shape complexity and Tree-Guided Pruning and Merging to eliminate redundant SVG nodes. L1-SAM-Prompting further recovers missing regions to improve recon- struction fidelity and structural completeness. Beyond reconstruction fidelity, we introduce a new evaluation protocol that measures structural quality and semantic part alignment. Experiments demonstrate that Lang-SVG achieves state-of-the-art performance in both rendering fidelity and structural quality. Project website: https://xiliu8006.github.io/Lang-SVG/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SpaceMCP: Vision Language Model Spatial Reasoning via Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15680v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. | **[TMT: Adapting CLIP to Infrared Imagery via Text-Derived Modality Knowledge Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12345v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models such as CLIP achieve strong performance across computer vision tasks, yet their image-text alignment is less reliable for infrared (IR) imagery because large-scale pre-training is dominated by visible-spectrum data. | **[Text Alone Predicts Which Classes a Segmentation Readout Keeps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11926v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An open-vocabulary segmentation readout silently loses a class deployed beyond its training categories. It still emits masks with high confidence, but the class never wins a single pixel. The training loss falls anyway, and mIoU can even rise while classes die. We show that which classes collapse is written in the class-name list alone. Before any image is seen, we rank each class's collapse risk from its text similarity to the training vocabulary alone. The ranking holds at AUROC 0.69-0.96 across nine model families, twelve backbones and eight pretraining recipes. Two further families, measured only after the predictions were written down, land in the same band. What the list writes, it can rewrite. With identical images and a class's counterpart moved in or out of the training list, retraining flips that class's gain over the frozen baseline by 21 IoU points; swapping in unrelated names changes nothing. We find a vocabulary-distance mechanism behind the collapse. Collapsed classes lose every vote to near-name neighbors, but in the frozen features they survive. Turning down the readout's amplitude, or stopping training early, brings them back, up to +16 points, without retraining; trained with its amplitude held down, the readout never collapses.</p></details> |  |
| **[RefineSplat: Reconstruction-Guided Object-Centric Gaussian Splatting for Open-Vocabulary Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10379v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary 3D scene understanding aims to segment and retrieve arbitrary objects in 3D scenes using natural language queries. | **[TerraMesh-Masks: Open-Vocabulary Segmentation for Earth Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08081v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary segmentation (OVS) enables models to segment a variety of concepts specified in natural language, removing the need for fixed label sets and fine-tuning. | **[LucidLyra: Feed-Forward Generative 3D Scene Reconstruction and Joint Understanding via Multi-Task Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40495v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present LucidLyra, a framework that makes generatively reconstructed 3D scenes directly queryable for semantics and instances in a feed-forward manner from video latents. | **[Understanding Temporal Semantic Stability in Open-Vocabulary UAV Perception through Metric 3D Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28665v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent open-vocabulary segmentation models have significantly advanced semantic perception for UAVs, for aerial and remote-sensing imagery, yet predictions from moving platforms can remain temporally inconsistent across repeated observations of the same physical scene. This behaviour is observed across two segmentation backbones and remains consistent under variations in voxel resolution, geometric association, and temporal sampling density. Overall, conditions that reduce world-space recurrence can increase apparent aggregate stability, demonstrating that semantic consistency must be interpreted together with observation support. | **[From Perspective to Fisheye Depth Estimation and Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27860v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision foundation models are capable of generalizing across 3-dimensional (3D) scenes with high-fidelity estimates; their empirical success can be attributed to training on large-scale datasets of perspective images. | **[TileShare: Training-Free Acceleration of Overlapping Tiled ViT Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32233v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fixed-window Vision Transformer (ViT) decoders run at deployment resolution as overlapping tiles: every token in an overlap band is processed by several full decoder instances. In the 2.4B-parameter MiniMax-H3 video decoder, about half of all per-token computation is duplicated. Removing the tiles fails because attention, global tokens and coordinates are bound to them. TileShare keeps each released tile's attention and computes every other operation once per token. The released computation changes at one kind of step only: an overlap token's per-tile results are blended into one shared state with the released weights. Interventions show that this blend costs quality where attention reads the shared states, mainly in the first few layers, which TileShare therefore keeps per tile. Without training, keeping six layers per tile decodes 1.38-1.51x faster than batched and compiled tiling on two GPU types (up to 1.96x with FP8) and is equivalent to the released decoder in PSNR, LPIPS and DISTS on DAVIS videos. On unseen UVG videos with the setting frozen beforehand, it matches PSNR and DISTS and improves LPIPS. TileShare carries over to two further decoders and is again confirmed on unseen data. It is equivalent in PSNR and DISTS on MAGI-1. On the ViTok-v2 image autoencoder at 2K-4K, it matches overlapping tiling in PSNR, improves LPIPS and DISTS and decodes 1.5-1.8x faster. How many layers stay per tile is measured once per model (for decoders, without labels); we report it for 14 decoders and encoders. Optionally, two GPU-hours of self-distillation let MiniMax-H3 share every layer without a loss in PSNR, and a coordinate field trained while the network stays frozen brings fully shared CLIP-based open-vocabulary segmentation to parity with sliding windows on seven benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main paper + supplementary material</p></details> |

| **[When to Adapt: Multi-Signal Domain Shift Detection for Efficient Training-Free Adaptation in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37602v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robust and reliable perception is essential for autonomous robots operating in real-world environments, particularly in long-term missions where environmental conditions may change significantly over time. Although recent advances in Visual Foundation Models (VFMs) have improved open-vocabulary semantic segmentation, these models can still suffer from domain shift, which can significantly degrade performance if they are not adapted to the current environment. Training-free domain adaptation is a relevant paradigm for adaptation, consisting of adjusting the model online using lightweight adapters. Recent approaches apply this on a per-frame basis, which is impractical for deployments on resource-constrained robotic hardware. To tackle this, we propose a multi-signal domain shift detection method for training-free continual test-time adaptation (TF-CTTA) in open-vocabulary segmentation. Our method leverages temporal coherence across consecutive frames by monitoring and combining complementary aspects of domain shift (visual change, adapter mismatch, and semantic drift) to trigger adaptation only when needed. We validate our approach on a benchmark including indoor and outdoor environments and using real robotic data. We demonstrate that our approach maintains segmentation accuracy while substantially reducing adaptations, making training-free adaptation practical and feasible for long-term, real-world robotic deployments.</p></details> |  |
| **[TACO: Training-free Sound Prompted Segmentation via Semantically Constrained Audio-visual CO-factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.01488v4)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale pre-trained audio and image models demonstrate an unprecedented degree of generalization, making them suitable for a wide range of applications. We adopt a different strategy: we introduce a training-free approach that leverages Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) to co-factorize audio and visual features from pre-trained models so as to reveal shared interpretable concepts. These concepts are passed on to an open-vocabulary segmentation model for precise segmentation maps. By using frozen pre-trained models, our method achieves high generalization and establishes state-of-the-art performance in unsupervised sound-prompted segmentation, significantly surpassing previous unsupervised methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[EviSplat: Preserving Multi-View Evidence in 3D Gaussian Splatting for Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34853v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary 3D scene understanding enables object localization and segmentation from free-form text queries without a fixed category vocabulary. However, observations of the same object vary across viewpoints and are not equally informative: some reveal cues relevant to a particular query, whereas others provide incomplete or misleading evidence. Pre-query consolidation can therefore suppress cues on which a later query depends. We introduce EviSplat, which preserves individual observation features as evidence for later text queries. EviSplat retains individual observation features within class-agnostic 3D instances that represent objects, object parts, or background regions. It also learns, for each Gaussian, a distribution describing which visual appearances its observations support. Given a text query, EviSplat scores each instance using its most relevant observations. It then computes a score for each Gaussian by combining instance-level relevance with locally supported evidence, weighted by how often and how unambiguously that Gaussian was observed. Different queries can thus draw on different visual cues from the same preserved evidence. Experiments across diverse datasets and evaluation protocols demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, supporting the benefit of preserving multi-view evidence until query time and aggregating it according to the query.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 7 figures, including appendix</p></details> |
| **[Printability-Constrained Adversarial Decals for Near-Nadir Aerial Perception: Measured Ink Gamuts, Nested Realism Constraints, and a Physical-World Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33513v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adversarial patches for aerial perception are typically evaluated as digital composites, with printing left as an implementation detail. This study imposes three physical constraints during optimization rather than after it: the color range a particular printer can reproduce, the size of the flat panel a vehicle offers, and the loss of fine detail incurred when the patch is imaged from altitude. The principal comparison isolates the ink set. Two patches share all seventeen recorded optimization settings and differ only in the colors available to them. One is constrained to a uniform color cube; the other to a gamut measured by printing and scanning a 216-patch chart. Each was optimized at three seeds and evaluated against thirteen victim conditions, with every rate reported against a size-matched optimized control. The effect of the measured gamut is victim-dependent rather than uniform. Net attack success rises on three of six closed-set segmentation victims, and for these the seed ranges of the two ink sets are disjoint: $+0.120$ on DeepLabv3-R101 and $+0.041$ on SegFormer-B0. The color-cube patch is consistently stronger on the open-vocabulary segmenter and on two of four detectors, though no detector exceeds a net of $+0.026$ under either ink set. The natural explanation is that a printable palette is simply less chromatic and lower in frequency than a digital one. Eleven further patches test this account and it does not hold. Once cardinality is matched, a palette as chromatic as the cube attacks equally well. Cardinality itself shows no trend from three inks to thirty-two. Palettes matched on cardinality, lightness and chroma, and differing only in hue placement, span $0.035$ to $0.136$. A physical evaluation with printed decals did not detect transfer; it bounds the transferred rate at $0.133$, which does not exclude the simulated value of $0.121$.</p></details> |  |

An instruction-conditioned perception mechanism then invokes open-vocabulary segmentation when the queried landmark is visible and its metric location can inform the next decision. | **[SatOV: Restoring Spatial Priors for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Segmentation in Remote Sensing Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22834v1)** | 2026-09-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVS) of remote sensing imagery is a challenging pixel-level task requiring strong generalization and adaptation to the spatial characteristics of remote sensing data. Specifically, Residual QQ Attention (ResQQ) extracts Query-Key self-attention from an intermediate CLIP layer and fuses it with final-layer Query-Query attention via a residual combination, restoring structural spatial priors suppressed by the final-layer representation. | **[ProClosure: Hierarchical Room-Object Assignment using Progressive Boundary Closure from Monocular Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12614v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A 3D scene graph groups objects into rooms. When a robot is asked to fetch an object from the kitchen, that grouping is what tells it where to look. An object recorded in the wrong room is not retrievable by a query naming the correct room. We introduce Progressive Boundary Closure, which recovers room layer from a monocular RGB video. A SLAM front end and an open-vocabulary segmenter supply a structural point cloud, camera trajectory and object tracks. The cloud is rasterised into a top-down map, rooms are recovered from it, and each object takes the room holding most of its extent. The difficulty lies in the map itself. Walls are recorded only where the camera looked, so a gap in the boundary may be a doorway or a stretch of wall that was never observed; nothing distinguishes the two. Prior methods treat both as passages, merging rooms that should remain separate. We observe that both require the same treatment: a room should not extend across either, so both are closed and need not be distinguished. Such an opening closes under a small amount of boundary growth, and few sightlines cross it, so points in different rooms rarely see one another. We use the first to recover rooms and the second to assign objects to them. Rooms are obtained by Progressively thickening the boundary inward and freezing each free-space region once it becomes enclosed, so every opening seals at its own scale rather than at a radius fixed in advance. Camera poses are used as seeds, which removes the sampling heuristic and makes the segmentation deterministic. Over 10 floors of 6 HM3D-Semantics scenes, scored against HOV-SG on identical top-down maps, we recover 74 rooms for 72 annotated regions (HOV-SG: 44), raising room F_1 from 0.741 to 0.890 at IoU 0.25 at some cost in precision, and object-to-room ARI from 0.488 to 0.696 (p=0.002, ahead on every floor).</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/ClarityLab-Org/ProClosure</p></details> |

| **[MAVISEG: Manifold Propagation and Visual Prototypes for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Segmentation in Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05878v1)** | 2026-08-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-image diffusion transformers learn about objects and scenes by learning to generate them, making them strong candidates for training-free zero-shot open-vocabulary semantic segmentation. Our first adaptation is task-aware: a Dynamic Expert Router (DER) that sparsely activates the most relevant experts by jointly reasoning about the visual input and the textual concept prompt, mimicking a clinical consultation process. Our second adaptation is parameter-aware: a Decomposed Parameterized Experts (DPE) design that represents each expert as a shared frozen base (inherited from the pretrained SAM3) and a lightweight trainable low-rank delta, reducing MoE parameter overhead by over 80\%. | **[Open-Vocabulary and Referring Segmentation for 3D Gaussians Using 2D Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30638v1)** | 2026-06-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged at the forefront of 3D scene reconstruction. | **[DLGStream: Dynamic Language-embedded Guassian Splatting for Open-vocabulary Enabled Free-viewpoint Video Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28840v1)** | 2026-06-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting~(3DGS) has emerged as a promising paradigm for reconstructing streamable free-viewpoint video~(FVV) from multi-view videos. | **[SAM3-I: Segment Anything with Instructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04585v4)** | 2026-04-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM3) advances open-vocabulary segmentation through promptable concept segmentation, enabling users to segment all instances associated with a given concept using short noun-phrase (NP) prompts. In this work, we present SAM3-I, an instruction-following extension of the SAM family that unifies concept-level grounding and instruction-level reasoning within a single segmentation framework. Built upon SAM3, SAM3-I introduces an instruction-aware cascaded adaptation mechanism with dedicated alignment losses that progressively aligns expressive instruction semantics with SAM3's vision-language representations, enabling direct interpretation of natural-language instructions while preserving its strong concept recall ability. To enable instruction-following learning, we introduce HMPL-Instruct, a large-scale instruction-centric dataset that systematically covers hierarchical instruction semantics and diverse target granularities. Experiments demonstrate that SAM3-I achieves appealing performance across referring and reasoning-based segmentation, showing that SAM3 can be effectively extended to follow complex natural-language instructions without sacrificing its original concept-driven strengths. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/debby-0527/SAM3-I.</p></details> |  |

We introduce two key optimizations: Precise Tile Intersection to reduce rasterization redundancy, and a novel Top-K Hard Selection strategy. This strategy leverages the geometric sparsity inherent in the 2D Gaussian representation to greatly simplify feature accumulation and alleviate bandwidth limitations, achieving render rates exceeding 40 FPS. Fast-SegSim provides critical value in robotic applications: it serves both as a high-frequency sensor input for simulation platforms like Gazebo, and its 3D-consistent outputs provide essential multi-view 'ground truth' labels for fine-tuning downstream perception tasks. | **[Towards Context-Aware Image Anonymization with Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27817v3)** | 2026-04-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Street-level imagery contains personally identifiable information (PII), some of which is context-dependent. We present an agentic framework CAIAMAR (\underline{C}ontext-\underline{A}ware \underline{I}mage \underline{A}nonymization with \underline{M}ulti-\underline{A}gent \underline{R}easoning) for context-aware PII segmentation with diffusion-based anonymization, combining pre-defined processing for high-confidence cases with multi-agent reasoning for indirect identifiers. The agentic workflow detects non-direct PII instances across object categories, and downstream semantic segmentation is preserved. Operating entirely on-premise with open-source models, the framework generates human-interpretable audit trails supporting EU's GDPR transparency requirements while flagging failed cases for human review.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to IEEE CVPR 2026 GRAIL-V Workshop</p></details> |
| **[OV-Stitcher: A Global Context-Aware Framework for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08110v2)** | 2026-04-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training-free open-vocabulary semantic segmentation(TF-OVSS) has recently attracted attention for its ability to perform dense prediction by leveraging the pretrained knowledge of large vision and vision-language models, without requiring additional training. | **[RADSeg: Unleashing Parameter and Compute Efficient Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Segmentation Using Agglomerative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19704v2)** | 2026-04-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) underpins many vision and robotics tasks that require generalizable semantic understanding. | **[Self-Calibrated CLIP for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.15869v3)** | 2026-03-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advancements in pre-trained vision-language models like CLIP have enabled the task of open-vocabulary segmentation. | **[PE3R: Perception-Efficient 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.07507v2)** | 2026-03-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in 2D-to-3D perception have enabled the recovery of 3D scene semantics from unposed images. | **[dinov3.seg: Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation with DINOv3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19531v1)** | 2026-03-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation (OVSS) assigns pixel-level labels from an open set of text-defined categories, demanding reliable generalization to unseen classes at inference. | **[ReLaGS: Relational Language Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17605v1)** | 2026-03-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Achieving unified 3D perception and reasoning across tasks such as segmentation, retrieval, and relation understanding remains challenging, as existing methods are either object-centric or rely on costly training for inter-object reasoning. Empirically, OVerSeeC handles novel entities, respects ranked and compositional preferences, and produces routes consistent with human-drawn trajectories across diverse regions, demonstrating robustness to distribution shifts. This shows that modular composition of foundation models enables open-vocabulary, preference-aligned costmap generation for scalable, mission-adaptive global planning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website : https://amrl.cs.utexas.edu/overseec/</p></details> |
